tbmm-2019-01-16sit01spe0003par0025	Thank you, Mr. Günay. The second non-responsive statement belongs to Ankara MP Ayhan Altıntaş, who wants to address the anniversary of the Defence Industry Undersecretary. Here you go, Mr. Goldstone. tbmm-2019-01-16sit01spe0004par0031	Mr. President, I have taken an off-the-book promise regarding the establishment of the Defense Industry Undersecretary in Turkey. I salute you all. It was founded in November 1985 by Law 3238 as the head of the Defence Industry Development and Support Administration, and was named the Defence Industry Undersecretary in 1989. The institution was launched on 13 January 1986, when the first Undersecretary, Mr. Vahit Erdem, was appointed. Last week it completed its 33rd year. In this speech, I would like to present information on the short history of the defence industry in Turkey, which I have prepared with the valuable contributions of Mr. Vahit Erdem. Mustafa Kemal Ataturk, despite very limited opportunities for economic development and the development of domestic industry from the founding of the republic, was of great importance. The development of defence industry in particular has been one of its major goals. Starting with the 1920s, the tank maintenance and renovation facility, the helicopter assembly and maintenance facility, various repair and maintenance workshops, the Lake Shipyard and the Styrofoam Shipyard, have been established. Also, light weapons and artillery repair shops, shell factories, munitions factories, various explosive facilities, artillery and rifle production facilities have been established. The General Directorate of Military Factories, which would later become the Institute of Machine and Chemical Industries, was established in the 1920s. In 1925 Tayyare and Motor Turk Anonymous were formed and planned to produce 250 aircraft annually with the German Junkers company. In this framework, the production of aircraft and motors in Kayseri was aimed at repairs and maintenance services in Eskişehir. In Kayseri, 24 aircraft were produced by the American company and 45 aircraft companies and Poland. During the Ataturk period, the Turkish Taiyare Society was founded in 1925 -- the Turkish Air Agency today -- and the Turkish Bird Flight School in 1935. Special initiatives are also important in the development of aviation industry in the early years of the Republic. The Vecihi Freebird produced its own Vecihi-X15 aircraft. Nu at the Thai factory where Nuri Demirağ founded. He built a plane he developed with a D-36 nickname. Unfortunately, these initiatives did not provide technological development and continuity because they did not receive the necessary support. Turkey became a NATO member in 1952. With NATO membership, the Turkish Armed Forces began modernising with NATO assistance. NATO's aid for military weapons, equipment and equipment also led Turkey to rehab. This period of recession and rehab lasted until the beginning of the 1963 Cyprus events and Operation 1974. The first move was launched in the early 1970s by the foundations of the Turkish Armed Forces. The Air Force Foundation established the Turkish Airlines Industry (TUSAS), the Land Force Foundation (ASELSAN) in 1975 to produce domestic aircraft in the 1970s. Today's Adapazari Tank Palet Factory was launched as the Palet Renewal Workshop in 1973. The established law of SSM38 aims to develop a modern defence industry and to develop local resources for modernisation of the Turkish Armed Forces, and to achieve those goals, the Defense Industry Support Fund, which has significant income outside the budget, has been established. The first project to move from the Turkish Armed Forces to the SSM was an armored combat vehicles project that would increase the operational capabilities of the Army Command. The project was carried out in Ankara Lakebaş, through the NUROL-FMC partnership. The F16 fighter aircraft and engine factories were then quickly completed with support from the SSM Fund, and the TAI in Ankara started producing TEI in Eskişehir. Later production was diversified by projects such as the initial training plane and light transport aircraft. In addition, the F16 electronic warfare system was developed by a local company, MICES, with a partnership with the US LORAL company. Similarly, the HF/SSB Jumping Radio Project is a project launched with a mobile radar complex, domestic and foreign company partnership. One of the most important steps in defence industry towards the late 1980s was the founding of ROKETSAN. In addition to industrialisation, defence-enhancing systems have been provided, such as general purpose helicopters, Super Cobra armed helicopters, and multiple-barrelled rocket launchers, which the Turkish Armed Forces desperately need.

tbmm-2019-01-16sit01spe0061par0284	Sir, I salute your grand delegation. As you know, in 1983, the current processing side of the balance of payments was liberalised in Turkey. Imports, exports, unseen transactions have been transferred to our commercial banks and private sectors under government control. After that, in 1989, the capital movement side of the balance of payments was liberalised, and from that day on, capital movements were allowed to enter and exit our country again under the control of the relevant units of our state. The balance of payments is an economic data set that monitors transactions between residents in Turkey and residents abroad through a double registration system. In recent years, we have seen capital outputs from our country getting faster and faster in the last two or three years, and capital intakes getting smaller. When we say capital and capital coming out of our country, of course, they're not dissimilar, they have sub-components. One of them is the exports of capital directly to create jobs, to buy factories in the country; the money the residents sent abroad to buy property abroad; on the other hand, the capital we imported, the capital they exported, the capital they exported, the foreign capital investments, portfolio investments, the union loans our banks took from abroad, and the loans our private sector took from abroad. All of this comes from instruments used to finance the current deficit in our country. When we look at the quality of the Cari deficit in financing, we see a significant improvement from 2003 to 2006. Cari, it turns out that we finance a significant portion of the deficit directly in the form of foreign capital investments in our country, with capital, but recently -- and I mean especially since 2014 -- there's a significant amount of capital coming out of our country -- when we look at it in the train. There could be two major reasons for this capital output. One of them may be in order to make the exports of Turkey easier, to increase and thus increase Turkey's gains by using international market opportunities, resources, and capital in countries where they are moving capital. This is the right attitude to be very supportive. A second issue may be that the capital in Turkey has faced some administrative difficulties, legal problems inside, or the problems that are thought to be facing in the future, the loss of trust, and, depending on that lost trust, it can take care of itself and raise its capital in places where it can feel safer and manage its assets in Turkey. tbmm-2019-01-16sit06spe0343par0293	Mr. President, the esteemed MPs, the European Accreditation Union and the International Laboratory Accreditation Association, announced their multilateral recognition agreements in 2007. Article 64, which was established by the presidential decree of the presidential office, was added to the law proposal by the Commission. According to this article, the Hellal Acreditation Foundation has been appointed as the only institution in Turkey to act as the only authority to act as an agent for the appropriate fitness assessment agencies. However, on the 27th article recently adopted, the Turkish Accreditation Agency has been appointed as the only authority to act as the sole authority to promote all appropriate assessment activities. As long as there is no clear definition of which products are subject to the legal fitness of the law, there will be a legal issue between the two institutions. In addition, Article 64 appears to be an anti-matter arrangement. A new institution is being uncovered with the 64th article and an amendment to Article 27 is being made, while the 27th article states that it cannot be used by other institutions. Mr. Minister of Trade has stated that 1.8 billion Muslim populations have been increasingly demanding for the documentation of helical products in food, textiles, pharmaceutical products, cosmetics, and the establishment of the halal Akreditation institution for this need to be met. In Western countries, the institution, non-profit or private companies are responsible for the accretion. In Egypt, which has the Muslim majority, the Standardization and Quality Organization, the Institute for Normalisation in Morocco, and the United Arab Emirates, the units of Standardization and Measurement Authority have been issued a legal certification, similar to the case in Malaysia. In our country, the Turkish Accreditation Agency, which has already been established with the 27th article on which we are discussing it, and has been emphasised that it is the only one in the matter of Akreditation, is the same bag of legislation and the Hellal Acreditation Agency is still the only authority to grant its legal certification. These arrangements create a contradiction between two articles, both indiscriminately and unnecessary red tape with a new institution. On the other hand, the two-headed arrangement contradicts the Charter of Accreditation and Market Surveillance Relations to Marketing of Products prepared by the European Union.
